Actually this idea is not that stupid. In order to be electrocuted, you need to make a complete circuit for the electricity to flow through you. For example, if I hang from a power line, as long as I do not touch the ground or some metal thing in the ground, I will not be electrocuted. As such, if the ninja dude were to jump onto the drone, and touch it in such a way as to not make a complete circuit, he would be fine.

There were two guys in a dumper truck dropping off some soil. One of them got out of the truck to watch as the other one tilted the back of the truck up to dump the soil. Unfortunately he was under some power lines and the back of the truck touched them. Sparks started flying everywhere. The guy outside the truck desperately tried to open the door and free his mate. The irony is, the guy inside the truck was fine as the truck was insulated from the ground by its rubber tyres. The guy who opened the door, was far from fine; ZAP!

#122913 15 2005-12-14T17:09:54Z Teh Dude
I have a better method of detecting snipers.
1) Record the average rating for each map (this is already done).
2) For each map and each user, record the difference between the average rating and the rating given by the user.
3) Calculate the root mean square of these differences for each user.

You now have a measure of how far away from the average these guys are rating maps.
#123016 7 2005-12-15T00:04:32Z Teh Dude
When creating N maps, it is possible to place gold on top of other gold to form a stack. However, once you have placed it, it is impossible to tell how many bits of gold are in the stack. The suggestion is that you should be able to place gold then set how many pieces are in that stack. So I would place the gold then type the number "10" if I want there to be 10 pieces in that position.

The original poster has called this number a flag. This is perhaps misleading as generally a flag is a boolean variable.
#123018 23 2005-12-15T00:10:40Z Teh Dude
Quote: sweep, Dec 14 2005, 05:11 PM
What if the majority of voters are wrong and the minority are right? You're assuming it's the other way round.

I am assuming that ON AVERAGE it is the other way round. For my assumption to fail, the majority of maps voted on by an individual would have to have an incorrect majority rating. If this were the case, then the map ratings would all be meaningless and could safely be ignored.

All averages work this way. I mean the map rating is an average right? If we were to work the other way around, then should we calculate some inverse average?

Besides, my method is tonnes better than just averaging an individual's ratings. As someone else has astutely pointed out, a user who rates either 1 or 5 will be the same as someone who rates evenly from 1 to 5.

#123291 9 2005-12-16T01:29:49Z Teh Dude
You could have weighted voting. So if someone writes a long review, their rating counts for more than someone who leaves no comment whatsoever. Of course this is open to abuse (copy and paste from wikipedia?). So you would need some kind of AI to read the reviews and check them for sense. Alternatively you could have reviewer ratings such as those on Amazon.

However, you would then need to leave comments when you rate a reviewer. Some people would abuse this by not leaving a comment, so you would need to have a system of weighted voting for the reviewer ratings. But then you would have to review these reviews of the reviewers. (Continues ad infinitum.)

#123308 35 2005-12-16T02:19:49Z Teh Dude
You say that "happy holidays" is a one-size-fits-all greeting. I conjecture that, in fact, it is not. I suggest that perhaps there is a point at which you stop going out of your way to try to avoid offending others. There is a balance to be struck.

Personally, I find it ridiculous that a corporation would wish me a happy/merry anything. If the staff in the store spontaneously decide to say something to me, then fine. However, how sincere can a corporate directive to wish everyone "happy holidays" be?

Of course the employees are representing the store and the corporation will be liable if any customers are offended by the employees. This is a shame.

What occurs to me is that it would probably be best to subscribe to every religion. That way, you could enjoy all of the available holidays. In addition to this, whichever holiday greeting you received, it would be apt. All the praying and stuff would get a little dry though.

To iangb, I would say that the point is that the vast majority of people are not offended by "Merry Christmas". Of those few who are offended, only a few will be seriously aggrieved. (I believe I made this point way back in this thread.)

Ultimately, everything that anyone does will probably be offensive to someone else at some point; see chaos theory and all that jazz. The key is to strike a balance. Blue Tetris has stated that his marketing group have decided that the balance lies with saying "Happy Holidays". For someone living in the deep south of America, the balance may be with a "Merry Christmas". I know that here in the UK, saying "Happy Holidays" would make you sound like some kind of idiot. It would not be appreciated by anyone and, as such, people will not want to say it.

If everyone eliminated all actions that may be offensive to others, then there would be no interaction whatsoever. If I go up to a hot girl and ask her for a date, and she has a boyfriend, then I will offend her boyfriend. However, until I go and speak to her I will not know that. I could play it safe and say nothing, but where is the fun in that?

#124867 90 2005-12-19T22:17:50Z Teh Dude
Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
This is true. However, the argument doesn't lie in how many people would indeed be aggrieved, but on how to minimize those who are, regardless of number.
Incorrect. The original point made by BT is that a store clerk, when presented with a customer needs to make a decision on which greeting to use. We have generalised this from store clerks to the general population. The overall aim is to minimise the total offense to others while maintaining your own happiness. Total number of offendees is crucial as more offendees generally implies more offense.

With this in mind, I will now reiterate:
Not that many people are offended by "Merry Christmas". Of those few who are offended, very few will be largely offended. As such, the total offense caused by using the greeting "Merry Christmas" is not large enough to offset the cultural shift that will be required by individuals who are used to using "Merry Christmas".

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
To say that it wouldn't be appreciated by "anyone" is a ridiclulous over-generalization, and I would like for you to retract that part.
Absolutely, "anyone" is an overgeneralisation. The vast majority would not appreciate it and would prefer (or at least be less confused) to hear "Merry Christmas".

However, the argument still stands. Please give me your thoughts on this with "anyone" replaced by "many people at all".

The interaction point is not entirely serious. However, it is true that the only way to reduce offense to others to an absolute zero is by not interacting with anyone whatsoever. In fact, you would probably have to go even further and eliminate all people.

By way of example, there exist some people for whom any kind of season's greeting is abhorrent. In this instance, saying either "Merry Christmas" or "Happy Holidays" will be offensive. As such the best option is to say nothing at all to this type of people. The only way to ensure that, when presented with a random individual, you do not offend them is to not greet them.

You have warped the boyfriend allegory to your own ends. I did not say that I knew that the girl had a boyfriend. Perhaps he has gone to the bar to get drinks, subsequently I walk in, see the girl alone and approach her. He meanwhile is watching. I am oblivious to him, if I knew he existed (ie that she had a boyfriend) I would not approach her. Offense is still caused.

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
I think we have a mismanagement of morals going on here. To those of you who don't wish to say "Happy Holidays," it's not a matter of you actually having any dislike for saying it, it's more of a personal problem that you have with "Not wanting to," or "sounding like an idiot," or "there being no fun in saying 'Happy Holidays'," all three of which are invalid reasons.
Here you reduce my entire point that the statement "Happy Holidays" is meaningless in the UK to the phrase "not wanting to sound like an idiot". "Happy Holidays" is meaningless and will make you look like an idiot (so not strictly meaningless as it displays idiocy). People do not want to look like idiots.

Finally, on what basis do you call this a "mismanagement of morals"? Please tell me the set of ethics at work here?

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
Stores are stores, and ALL stores should take into account ALL belief systems, regardless of personal preference.
Do you actually believe this? Because that would be a store of epic proportions. Not to mention it would render all other stores redundant. Could you really see Palestinians and Israelis shopping side by side? Not to mention issues such as Halal meat.

Specialist stores are required as there are specialist items. Turbans, Kippahs and dog collars are hardly everyday items.

Now for the real meat...

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
1. 'Merry Christmas' operates as a predominantly Christian greeting.
FALSE Many, many atheists celebrate Christmas. To me, Christmas has absolutely no tie to religion. It is just a time to see family, eat Turkey and exchange gifts.

I would imagine that there are many other non-Christians who celebrate Christmas also.

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
2. 'Happy Holidays' operates as a universal greeting.
FALSE I have already given examples of when "Happy Holidays" fails. 1) If the individual has no holidays. 2) If the individual is offended by any type of seasonal greeting. 3) If the individual has no recognition of "Happy Holidays" (for example they are British)

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
3. 'Merry Christmas' may offend people of the denominations involved in Judaism, Islam, Buddhism, Taoism, Atheism, Shintoism, Taoism, and other faiths not related to Christianity.
This is true but the argument does not follow. The fact that some people may be offended (and to what extent?) does not mean that it is worth abandoning "Merry Christmas". People can be offended by the smallest look, but I will not stop looking at people.

Quote: Kablizzy, 2005-12-19T21:00:51Z
4. 'Happy Holidays' will not offend people of any denomination ("offend" being used in the purely literal sense, not in the sense that 'people will look at you funny.'), since it is a universal greeting, intended to include all people in a single phrase.
This is just point 2 rehashed. See above.

Then all the Jewish peeps should just tolerate hearing "Merry Christmas" all of the time.

(btw that was another example of a so-called straw-man argument)

Tolerance only gets you so far. Adapting your behaviour to suit your company also only gets you so far. Adopting a single mode of behaviour (saying "Happy Holidays" as your greeting), only gets you so far.

The motivation for saying "Happy Holidays" is to wish the other person well in an attempt to ingratiate you and, by extension, your company, to them. In some cases, eg in the majority of cases in the UK, they will just see you as some wacko. The entire excercise will fail in its aims.

#133194 5 2006-01-17T17:43:21Z Teh Dude
This is fairly interesting. It seems that mastering N is really just about mastering the ability to determine then perform a perfect sequence of key presses. I will call this predictive.

Other games, such as first person shooters (think CounterStrike, Day of Defeat), are far more reactive.

I think this probably has to do with the amount of determinism in the game. N is more or less completely deterministic, nothing unpredictable happens. In online FPSs, there are other humans playing. As such, the game is not deterministic.

Also having other humans (or intelligent adversaries of some sort) adds a dimension of tactics; you must outwit your opponents.

N teaches you an important lesson as death is always your fault. In other games you may have just been outskilled or been unlucky - in N there is no one to blame but yourself.

#135051 125 2006-01-23T15:40:03Z Teh Dude
I don't really think it is fair to say that a PC is easier to use. Admittedly, Ctrl-Click is a bit convoluted but I believe the point of having a single button is so as to avoid confusing Left/Right click instructions. Believe it or not but people who are new to computers often get confused by this.

Macs have been designed to be easy to use. They work right out of the box, networking just works, CD ripping just happens when you place the disk in the drive, etc.

It was Apple who pioneered the WIMP (Windows, Icons, Menus, Pointers) interface in a commercial system. They also advocated that all programs for their operating system must adhere to style guidelines. These govern things such as the order of items in menus and such like. This is in an effort to keep the user experience uniform between different applications, making the whole system easier to use.

Of course Windows now have these same features but overall I feel that a Mac is easier to use. I believe that the reason that people think that Macs are hard to use is that they are not used to them. If I were to place a Mac and a Dell (both still in their boxes) in a room with two identical old people, the old person with the Mac would be able to get on the Internet the quickest.

As a final note, most of what I have addressed above is usability for n00bs. Experienced users need speed and desire things such as shortcut keys; I believe that these are present in both XP and OSX.

#135784 30 2006-01-25T18:02:53Z Teh Dude
Quote: pariah, Jan 25 2006, 05:32 PM
I'll try and find a good example of this; If anyone else has one, please post.
The concept of an Earth-centred Ptolemaic universe that was widely held by the Catholic church in the 1500s until Copernicus came along and blew it out of the water. (Google for Ptolemy and Copernicus for more info.)

There is a long history of churches opposing scientific discoveries that contradict their current beliefs. However, once the science has matured to the point that it is almost incontrovertible to anyone with any sense, the church has to give in or else look outmoded and backward. If the Catholic church were still to believe in a Ptolemaic universe, then it would cast doubt on all of their other beliefs (an omnipotent god, etc).
#135786 8 2006-01-25T18:14:03Z Teh Dude
During WWII there was a hare-brained scheme to use pigeons as guidance systems for bombs and missiles. The pigeons were trained to peck pictures of military installations; each time they pecked the correct part of the image, they would be rewarded with some bird seed.

Special bombs were prepared with a cradle to retain the pigeon placed in the nosecone. The pigeons had a little window to the outside world that was rigged up in such a way that pushing on different parts of the window caused the bomb's tail fins to change their angle such that the bomb moved towards the direction in which the window was pushed. So, with a pigeon pecking at the window, the bomb would move toward whatever it was they were pecking.

I believe that the dude behind this scheme was called Skinner.

#135846 45 2006-01-25T21:07:34Z Teh Dude
The entire point of this thread is that religion is not science.

You can carry out scientific experiments but still be religious. Science can provide answers in so much as it provides models of the real world which can be used to explain and predict things that happen.

Religion cannot produce new theories and cannot be used to make useful predictions about the world. Religion can make people happy though and can be used as a basis for morals.

Science is not "crude". What is crude about the scientific method? One of the tenets of science is that there is no absolute truth, you can never find a perfect theory. All theories are the best explanation based on the current evidence.

My birth was certain because I am here to say that. You say your self "now that you are born", so are you certain that I am/was born? If we can be certain about future events such as death, then surely we can be certain about past events such as birth. The statement is not "there is only one future event in my life which I can be certain about".

Of course, we could reason that I was not born; perhaps I magically appeared.

Anyway, it appears that some of you are confused. The statement is not "death is certain" but rather "only one thing is certain: your death". So to disprove this, we can either show that your (whoever that is) death is not certain or that something else is certain

PS Pariah I bet you are the type of person who argues that there must be life on other planets because there is life on Earth and what are the chances that Earth is the only place with life. Can you see why this is fallacious?

#138164 142 2006-02-01T12:48:39Z Teh Dude
Regarding Google Linux being spyware/adware.

First of all, Google is primarily a business. The aim of a business is to make money. Google make money by selling advertising. The niche that Google fills is directed advertising. They decide which adverts to show based on your behaviour. For the search engine, this is simply based on your current query. For Gmail, a bot reads your email and displays adverts based on this.

Google keeps all of your search queries until the year 2038. These search queries are all linked to a unique identifier and your IP address. More recently, they have started to link in further information from your Gmail account (if you have one). Personally, I do not like the idea of a money-driven business having a record of everything that I have searched for, a list of my email contacts and knowledge of the content of my emails.

If you extend still further and get a Google operating system then you really are opening the floodgate to privacy invasion.

#138440 14 2006-02-02T00:33:10Z Teh Dude
Here is my marvellous method:
1) Record the average rating for each map (this is already done).
2) For each map and each user, record the difference between the average rating and the rating given by the user.
3) Calculate the root mean square of these differences for each user.

You now have a measure of how far away from the average these guys are rating maps.
#138475 17 2006-02-02T01:58:54Z Teh Dude
@Atilla: That is the underlying assumption of having the map ratings. This measure is based on the assumption that in general maps are correctly rated. If the ratings really are all incorrect then why bother having them?

@Pariah: This measure just shows the average difference between a user's rating and the actual rating. How high/low the actual rating is does not affect this measure. So the fact that someone votes only for "good maps" has no bearing on this measure. It also does not tell you if a user is over-rating or under-rating maps. I suggest that you take another look at the method as it appears you have misunderstood slightly.

#138965 23 2006-02-03T12:39:50Z Teh Dude
Atilla that is a much better and far more constructive criticism.

As far as I see it, you have identified two problems with my so-called "marvellous method":
1) Having to recalculate each time a map's rating changes.
2) Having n00bs rate a map then someone else rating it correctly.

To solve problem 1 is simple. Just calculate the difference once, at the time of voting, and recalculate the RMS. Remember, the RMS is for all differences over every map that you have rated.

To solve your problem of an average being skewed if few people have voted (problem 2); simply have a floor value of votes, below which you do not use the difference for calculating the measure. You could also have a floor for if you have only voted on a few maps. So below 5 map votes, your RMS is not available. Or it is available but with the caveat that it is unreliable.

Explicitly, in your example, you are forgetting that your difference would be averaged. So yes you may have a difference in this circumstance of 3. However, averaged over many maps this difference would be reduced.

Finally, I am glad that you are lending such a constructively critical eye to this. Thanks.

#138970 42 2006-02-03T13:01:45Z Teh Dude
Viruses benefiting their creator: Yes they do. You can use a virus to create a bot net. You can then use this bot net to send spam email in return for cash or to DDoS websites in order to blackmail for cash.

My "only n00bs open dodgy email" comment: Sorry if this was offensive. As I am the sole user of my PC, I forgot that others have n00bish little brothers who do open such emails. These people are still n00bs though.

This virus proves that Windoze sucks: This virus does not exploit any inherent flaw in the Windows operating system. It relies on the user running a program with administration priviledges. Of course, most Windoze boxes are set to run with admin rights as default so this could be seen as a flaw in Windows.

For any of you worried about this type of exploit (especially those with n00b younger siblings) you should try to run in non-admin mode. This is one of the best things you can do in order to secure your PC. Best of all, it is free. Check out this site for more information.

Question: does Mac OSX run in admin or non-admin mode by default? It is based on UNIX which suggests that it doesn't run in root mode all the time but I would like to know from one of the Mac advocates.

#141134 33 2006-02-09T02:54:09Z Teh Dude
The appeal of a film is that you can passively observe another world. Generally films have much better stories than games. Plus films last less time than games and as such have more story content per minute. Also it is easier to portray your vision as a film director than it is in a computer game.

I am very much a substance-over-style person. As such, on the few occasions that I have played RPGs, I have stripped away the story and looked at the gameplay. Of course you can argue that games are not just about gameplay but about the experience as a whole. I find that good gameplay becomes immersive; I forget any distractions and just flow.

The only MMORPG that I have tried is RuneScape. To be honest, trekking across maps for minutes on end in order to retrieve an arbitrary collection of items is not my idea of fun. Sure, at higher levels you can teleport and whatnot, but I don't want to invest that much time performing repetitive tasks in order to get mediocre pay-off.

For those of you who do play RPGs/MMORPGs; when I play online FPSs like Quake II and Day of Defeat, I progress in terms of my skill at the game. My reactions sharpen, my aim is better, I learn tactics, etc. I would imagine that a similar thing happens when you play N a lot. When you play RPGs, you obviously get skill points for your characters. However, how do you as a person improve in terms of skill?

(Please note that I am not assuming that you don't get more skilled at RPGs, I just wish to know the nature of this skill.)

#142405 4 2006-02-11T23:38:28Z Teh Dude
I took that one from Steven Pinker's book on natural language called The Language Instinct. There is an animal called a buffalo. There is a place called Buffalo so a buffalo from Buffalo would be a Buffalo buffalo. There is also a verb [i]to buffalo[i], meaning to intimidate.

Filling in some words that express meaning but are not strictly required we get:

The Buffalo buffalo that buffalo Buffalo buffalo often buffalo in turn buffalo Buffalo buffalo.

This guy has attempted to prove that you can repeat the word buffalo an arbitrary number of times.
#142419 13 2006-02-11T23:57:59Z Teh Dude
Quote: smartalco, Feb 11 2006, 11:20 PM
why really long?!?, in a ip in the form of ###.###.##.### there are several million combinations, the wireless router at my house has an ip in that form, and the 3 laptops/2 desktops that run through there all have different ip's, just the last # differs
Right. Time for a quick lesson on IP addresses. There are three classes that are normally used, A, B and C (D is for multicast and E is not used). All IP addresses are 32 bits long.

Class A addresses start with 0, followed by 7 bits specifying the network and 24 bits specifying the host.
Class B addresses start with 10, followed by 14 network bits and 16 host bits.
Class C addresses start with 110, followed by 21 network bits and 8 host bits.

The reason that the IP address is split like this is so that you can route data around the Internet more easily. If the address space was flat, ie all 32 bits were for the host, then each router on the Internet would have to store an entry for every single address telling it where to send data for that address. With this system, you for the first 0 bit which tells you what class the address is. Then you use the next 7, 14 or 21 bits to choose where to send the data.

So you can have 2^7 networks each with 2^24 computers attached; 2^14 each with 2^16 computers; and 2^21 each with 2^8 computers. (These numbers are not quite correct as some addresses are "special".)

Now, if I own a huge network of computers, and I am given a class A address, then I get a set of addresses of the form
1 <7 digits unique to my address> <24 bits which are different for each computer>.
Unless I have 2^24 computers, some addresses are going to go to waste. The same goes for class B and class C. This means lots of addresses are left unused. Also bear in mind that a single person can have multiple PCs. Also it is not just PCs but servers and things like mobile phones and other embedded devices that use IPs these days.

Now we get to home networks. You have a set of IP addresses for your local area network (LAN), these can be anything you like as they are not visible to the outside world. Each computer has a different one, but I would imagine the first part is the same for each computer. Your router or broadband modem or whatever you have, has another IP address assigned by your ISP. This is what the outside world sees as your IP. In fact, often the outside world will have no idea about your home network, to all intents and purposes there is just one computer with an IP.

#142792 2 2006-02-12T23:06:46Z Teh Dude
What kind of Ethernet cable are you using between the laptop and PS2? It needs to be a twisted pair one so that traffic can flow both ways (as you are not using a hub or switch). You will also have to bridge the Airport connection and the ethernet connection (I am not sure how to do this on Macs).

Otherwise, you will have to get a device that can receive Airport signals and has an ethernet port for your PS2. Apple have Airport Extreme base stations but these cost $$$.

#144673 769 2006-02-16T22:31:26Z Teh Dude
Bear in mind that the sequence in which the numbers appear does not matter.

For those of you who are a little dense, let me explain why this is. You get a new number every time you load the page. This implies that the numbers are being generated by a script. The script generates a new number based either on the last number or on the time or at random (either totally random or picked randomly from a list). Now all of these options, except for the first one, would produce numbers that are not in any order. The first option produces numbers in order but there are loads of people loading up NUMA each second.

So if I have my magic sequence 1 1 2 3 5 8 13 21. Say kai loads NUMA first. He gets 1. But then kirby loads it and takes the next 1. Wedgie comes along and gets 2. Now when kai loads the site he gets 3. He comes back later and gets 13.

So kai has 1,3,13 and he thinks "wtf is this pile of <expletive deleted>".

The only way you could get the numbers in sequence is with cookies so the script knows how far you are with the sequence and can give you a new number based on this. I tried deleting all numa cookies then revisiting the front page. No cookies were created.

A further fault with giving numbers in sequence is that even if kai was given 1 1 2 3 5 8 13 21, what would happen if he forgot one of the numbers or if he pressed reload twice by accident?

I hope that this explains why the sequence in which the numbers appear does not matter.

Quote: Minitrue
This message has been deemed doubleplusungood crimethink by Minitrue.  You know, thirty years ago we didn't have crimethink at all.  Things were simpler.  A Ministry knew his place in the world.  Of course, this was all before the War.  I was in the war.  Did I ever tell you that?  Private Minitrue.  Heh heh.  That brings back memories.  I remember when we first touched foot on enemy soil.  We saw the jungle just a few clicks ahead and the sergeant called for us all to partner up and search for enemies.  'Course I went with Leo.
Leo and I were like brothers. He was nearly bald.  He had an effeminate look about him.  He was narrow and tall, standing about six and a half feet.  He didn't have proper diction but that didn't matter.  He was rather intuitive and I felt safe with him.
When we ventured out into the darkness of the jungle, everything became dead silent.  Leo looked over at me and nodded.  He was taking the lead.  I readied my rifle and followed closely behind.  The air was thick soup.  Rain began to fall; it picked up quickly and became a raging tempest around us.  Despite the storm, the world was quiet.  The rain filled the world with a solid noise, like that of loud fan.  Nothing in the jungle moved.  It was a single frame of reality drawn out into hours.  We ventured for nearly half a day in a humming, motionless world.  It was beyond surreal.  It was like nothing I had ever seen.  There was a matte-like glow to the jungle, as if it were filled with fluourescent lights.  My world seemed fake.
After a time, Leo reclined against a rock and said we ought to rest.  I didn't feel tired.  I laid myself on the jungle bed.  It was hard like concrete.  My mind started wandering.  Would I ever make it back home?  Would I ever be able to see my kids again?  What would become of Miniluv in my absense?  I bunched some moss beneath me.  I laid my head upon the moss and began to slumber.  The sleep was fitful and felt eternal.  It was dreamless.  I could swear I was somehow conscious the whole time.
I awoke after an immeasurable length of time to gunfire in the north.  Leo was already awake.  He was chattering on the radio with the sarge.
"We gotta go, Mini," Leo shouted.
"Let's do this," I replied.

#144907 5 2006-02-17T16:13:51Z Teh Dude
Quote: iangb, Feb 17 2006, 11:27 AM
what the...

Trojans just come in and... mess up your computer. There are thousands of them. I didn't think they needed passwords - they just wait until a certain event, and then activate.
Trojans require admin privileges. That is why they are called Trojans - you must trust them in some way and actively take them into your system (ie run them with admin privileges). Now, XP by default runs with admin privileges which is stupid. So in this case all you need to do is run the Trojan. UNIX systems, eg Linux, do not run with admin privileges (only root has them), so if you try to run a program that requires admin privileges you are asked for a root password. Now, I am not sure what happens on OSX but it is based on UNIX so I would imagine that it does not by default run with admin privileges.

Got it?

#145022 8 2006-02-17T20:47:47Z Teh Dude
Do you really think that I didn't know that it comes from Trojan Horse? Seriously go and read about Trojans on Wikipedia if you don't believe what I am telling you. Go and talk to a senior lecturer at a top UK university.

Trojans must be executed by someone with privileged access to a computer in order for them to do anything malicious. Now I could make some kind of trojan that just deletes everything in C:\temp. Here it is: 'del C:\temp\*.*'. But if I want to mess with system files in any way (ie actually infect the system) then I need admin rights.

Referring to the Mac Trojan in this example - you are perfectly safe unless you execute the malicious code with admin rights.

The vast majority of exploits rely on this. There are very few true viruses which can infect your computer without you doing something stupid first. Case in point, I have never had a virus on my computer.

As a final point, your obvious lack of knowledge about computers is demonstrated by the fact that you think you can have code in an image. You might be able to craft a special image that causes a buffer overrun in your webbrowser which allows code stored somewhere else in memory to be executed while your CPU is in privileged mode but this is 1) not a Trojan (rather an exploit) and 2) not code.

#145198 10 2006-02-18T03:47:07Z Teh Dude
Quote: iangb, Feb 18 2006, 03:29 AM
Trojan horse exploits image flaw. So there.

An image is code, fool. As is everything on a computer.
No, an image is data. Everything on a computer is either code or data. Code consists of sets of instructions for your CPU, data is what is processed by those instructions. Seriously Ian, go and get a clue about computers then argue with me. If you don't believe me, then look here.

Now for your article, yes the reporter uses the term "Trojan horse images" but he is using it in very woolly way. If you actually investigate the exploit you will see that it is, exactly as I stated, a buffer overflow problem. It even says in there "The privileges gained by a remote attacker depend on the software component being attacked."

There is one image format that includes code as well as data. Namely WMF. You can find out about it here. However, you still have to open the image in order to infect your computer and "An attacker who successfully exploited this vulnerability could gain the same user rights as the local user. Users whose accounts are configured to have fewer user rights on the system could be less impacted than users who operate with administrative user rights." So you need to be in admin mode to get privileged access.

There is an insurmountable amount of evidence that says that I am right. Please just give up.

#145614 18 2006-02-19T06:56:43Z Teh Dude
Quote: TornadoTK, Feb 19 2006, 06:33 AM
Trojans generalize as maliciously-coded programs that are normally installed without permission (and more likely to be installed without any knowledge of it happening).

If I'm wrong, eh, so what. I think that's the perfect explanation of a trojan.

You are wrong. You have almost defined the opposite of a trojan.

The point of a trojan is that it requires the user to take action. The original trojans were just simple .exes that were named something like "Best Program Ever.exe". Users would open them and this would allow remote access to their PCs by malicious people.

The reason it is called a trojan horse is that it is not what it seems. So it looks innocent and innocuous yet there is a deadly payload inside. The reason it looks harmless is to fool you into taking it into your computer (i.e. executing it).

Again, can I refer you to the Wikipedia page on trojans: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_horse_(computing)

#146265 32 2006-02-20T19:55:09Z Teh Dude
Quote: clux, Feb 20 2006, 07:14 PM
Quote: Teh Dude, Feb 20 2006, 01:05 AM
What? Can you explain that to me please.
There's not really much to explain. It's an nonsensical retort which relies solely on another saying; there are no rules without exceptions.

edit:
quick google

Well actually, the point of that phrase is this. If I have a rule with special cases, I can use the special cases to define the rule. So if it says "parking prohibited on Sundays", I know that for Monday to Saturday it is OK to park. Here the entire rule would be "Parking all week except for Sundays" implicitly stated by giving just the exception "parking prohibited on Sundays".

The point of my question was to get you to explain how that phrase fits in with the current topic. It was a subtle way of telling you that you are being foolish by using a phrase where it does not apply.

#147546 34 2006-02-23T19:01:29Z Teh Dude
Quote: blue_tetris, Feb 23 2006, 06:42 PM
People don't do things because they're illegal.  That's a parental myth.  People do things because there is a perceived benefit.
I beg to differ. In a social group, visibly flaunting rules in front of others makes you look daring and powerful. Obviously this has a payoff. Doing something illicit with a group of your peers has a conspiratorial nature that solidifies bonds within that group and differentiates the group from others.

Teenagers do wish to rebel and will do things that have little benefit simply in order to do so. A prime example of such a thing is vandalism. What possible (direct) benefit is there in despoiling someone else's property?

Turning to other areas, there are a great number of people who like to have sex in public places. Despite the impracticalities they find this enjoyable because there is a risk of getting caught; they feel they are doing something wrong and/or naughty (indeed, often they are breaking the law).

I believe that within many people there is an inherent instinct to break rules. It is quite obvious to see how this could result from evolution.

pariah's point about heroin is probably due to the low availability and greater negative effects of that drug.

As a final point, I despise your response of "That's a parental myth". It is absolutely pathetic and would be akin to me saying "You smoked too much pot and it has addled your brain". (Whether or not you have smoked any pot, just as your comment was made whether or not I have sired any children.)

Quote: BT, 2006-02-23T16:11:02Z
"Its illegal status should reduce its incidence of usage. But it doesn't. http://www.norml.org/index.cfm?Group_ID=3383"
So either making it illegal increases its usage or leaves it the same. Making marijuana illegal makes it harder to get hold of so should decrease its usage; unless there is another factor at work, i.e. my "illicit is sexy" factor. Remember, I am not (yet) arguing either way on the decriminalise stance; I am simply arguing that making marijuana illegal makes it more appealing. In economic terms, making it illegal increases demand (at least for some individuals) but reduces supply.

The point about alcohol being illegal for kids is an interesting one. However, this falls down because marijuana is completely illegal at all levels. Marijuana is "more illegal" if you will. A lot of kids' parents drink alcohol but do not smoke pot so if they are caught drinking there will be less trouble than if they are caught getting high.

#148397 2 2006-02-25T14:30:42Z Teh Dude
It is a good idea. I think N could benefit from more timed actions. Currently there are ways of forcing timing (eg drones, thwump walls) but timed doors where you press a button then have X seconds to reach the exit would be good. This would mean that even if you fail to reach the exit, you can go back and try again.

Also, as you suggested, doors that open only after a certain amount of time. So you could have survival maps as part of the game.

The gold counter doors are a good idea too. Currently, there is not that much of a need to get gold. If you had to get at least Y pieces of gold to open the exit then this would force people to get more gold. Also you could distribute gold in different places so people would have a choice of which challenges to overcome. For example, one difficult area may contain lots of gold while there are multiple easier areas each with less gold. You could kind of achieve this by having doors, and people have to press Y buttons before they can reach the exit but this is messy.

In conclusion, all of these would make interesting additions as they add to gameplay/level design.

Possible words (just what comes to my mind, and words from the Internet Anagram Server):

episode, folder, lord, soil, solo, file, rifle, profile(s), fire(s), deed, oil
I suggest you try this software: http://www.anagramgenius.com/agfree.php

It generates anagrams with the added benefit that you can rate certain sub-words. This means you do not have to wade through all possible anagrams.

Again, remember that the anagram may contain names. Names will not be generated by computer programs that generate anagrams. If the anagram contains some huge name, then lots of those aforementioned words (eg episode, folder) will not be in the anagram.

"form episode lode"
#148452 1152 2006-02-25T16:15:21Z Teh Dude
IAS can't do that. IAS can have "must include this word". However that precludes lots of anagrams. Anagram Genius lets you decide how much you like each word that is contained in the letters. It then ranks the results according to which words you like/dislike.

Seriously, try Anagram Genius guys.

#148476 6 2006-02-25T16:57:16Z Teh Dude
Quote: Ngaged, Feb 25 2006, 02:36 PM
If someone just wanted to take a speed run through a level, it wouldn't be as fun for them because they'd HAVE to waiit for a door to open and they'd HAVE to collect gold.

First off, I don't think you necessarily need to have every door with both a timer and a gold counter. Secondly, the timer could be the other way round, so the door stays open for only a few seconds. This way, speed runners can still go as fast as they like/are able to. Thirdly, collecting gold quickly is still speed running. Fourthly, having doors that don't open until a certain amount of time has elapsed would be like the current survival maps which noone speed runs anyway. Finally, N is not all about speed running; the vast majority of players do not play for high scores.

#150681 4 2006-03-02T10:36:27Z Teh Dude
Quote: sweep, March 02, 2006 08:37 am
And you're blaming Bill Gates?

he created the PC

No he didn't! He wrote an operating system for PCs. Early versions of which sucked hard and were actually a step back from the current technology. Later versions ripped off Apple which had ripped off Xerox. The current versions are reasonable.

The area in which Bill Gates has excelled is not in creating a good operating system but rather in selling ridiculous numbers of that operating system and in making it incredibly hard to change to a different operating system. From a business point of view, this is great. From a user's point of view, it is not good. Monopolies harm consumers.

Finally, if a software update breaks your computer then you have a right to be angry. Software updates should not break things. If everyone just rolled over then those writing the software updates would have no incentive to ensure that they do not break peoples' computers.

#150811 33 2006-03-02T18:37:59Z Teh Dude
The force applied by the beam to the ninja would go as k/R^2 (like gravity) . Where R is the distance of the ninja from the turret and k is a constant. So it gets much stronger the closer you get. k could be positive or negative giving you either a push or a pull.

If you were pulled toward the centre, you could probably be accelerated enough that you would fly out the other side. Although I am not entirely sure. Some modelling would determine this.

Getting stuck could be a trap. I can imagine some crazy maps with loads of these tractor turrets with a small safe route that you have to negotiate. So I am not ruling out the possibility of getting stuck.

#150959 19 2006-03-03T00:20:21Z Teh Dude
smartalco: That is exactly what I just said. Except you are massively wrong about highscores. Where the hell do you think highscores come from? That's right: a highscore server on the Interweb. Unless the highscore server can automatically detect attempts to upload highscores and create new slots to hold highscores for new maps, then every single time someone creates a new map pack, someone would have to tinker with the server code. If the highscore server was automated, it would be totally uncontrolled as people could just submit thousands of new map packs and as such create a DoS attack.

So yeah, you could quite easily support user created episodes and even have personal bests for them. However, supporting highscores would be a far larger task involving elements beyond just the N client code.

#151305 57 2006-03-03T23:44:00Z Teh Dude
Quote: blue_tetris, March 03, 2006 11:03 pm
Having the pull increase when you near the object is cool for physics and all, but I don't think it's very practical for gameplay. Especially considering that the game would be absolutely no fun when the pull was the strongest and you were locked in the drone (and the pull would inevitably get to that point, because you are accelerating towards the object). Well now BT your lack of knowledge of mechanics is showing here. The tractor drone/turret applies a force which accelerates the ninja towards the drone/turret. At the point at which the ninja reaches the drurret, he will have some momentum and as such will pass through the other side. If the ninja also runs towards the drurret, there will be even more momentum and the ninja could well escape.

Of course, all of this depends on various factors, including but not limited to, the strength of the tractor beam and the direction in which it is pulling the ninja. I believe that earlier I suggested that some testing was in order. Of course, the testing may well show that a 1/R^2 relationship is not fun.

#151456 65 2006-03-04T11:55:57Z Teh Dude
Quote: Teh Dude, March 03, 2006 02:17 pm
Quote: tl0, March 03, 2006 12:34 pm
my opinion, and that is also the scientific view i think, is that everything is certain, because everything is predictable.
That is not the scientific view at all. Have you not heard of the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle?

Tsukatu you are having serious problems comprehending what I am writing. As far as I can see you are actually agreeing with me. I stated that due to the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle nothing is predictable (with 100% certainty). At that point, I was not saying anything about certainty other than that using predictability to prove certainty is fallacious as predictability is impossible due to the HUP.

You really have not given an explanation of why the HUP does not show that nothing can be completely predicted. Perhaps "completely predicted" is a bit of a weak term - by this I mean "predicted with 100% accuracy".

I am not sure how much quantum physics you have done Tsukatu, but there are many events which have more than one possible outcome.

Quote: Tsukatu, 2006-03-04T07:02:20Z
I really don't see a thing wrong with the idea that every bit of matter follows specific rules and so has only one outcome, predictable or not, and that happens to include neuron firings in the brain, which influence your thoughts and actions entirely without giving you a chance to pick a different set of neuron firings.
I need you to clarify "has only one outcome". Is that one possible outcome a priori? Of course, after the event you can observe the outcome and say ooh that was the one outcome. However before the event, you may know that there is going to be one outcome but not what that outcome is. There may be 1000 possible different outcomes and the event randomly selects one. If this were the case then you could observe the outcome of some event A, reset the Universe to just before A occurred and observe a different outcome from the event A. Of course, this is completely impossible in practice.

#151600 17 2006-03-04T19:54:52Z Teh Dude
Quote: Evil___, March 04, 2006 12:06 pm
iangb's got the point there. A pathfinding algo for drones works fine because the drones can only move in 4 directions. The ninja can move down to 1 pixel! The only way around this would be to have the map-maker program the AI themselves - something that might take ages and then would be just following a set path.

That is not why pathfinding is hard. In any given frame, there are only three things (excluding kill and boss mode) that the ninja (i.e. the player) can do; press left, press right, press jump. This gives three moves, while drones have four. The drones do not use pathfinding, they just move in a straight line until they hit a wall or detect the ninja.

The pathfinding difficulty is that the places you can get to from the current frame, depend on things like how fast the ninja is moving, in which direction, how long the jump button has been held for, etc. If the ninja was like a chess piece and moved in fixed jumps from square to square, then it would be easy to create a graph for path finding as this graph would be the same for all possible sequences of previous moves. In N, the possible next moves depend on what moves have been made in the past.

I have returned merely to set you on the path to enlightenment.

symlinks obviously will not work as n requires the 32bit versions of the files. You must find the .deb package containing the missing files. Once downloaded, you can use dpkg -x to extract the files. You must then copy them across to the lib32 folder.

Finding the packages can be a little tricky. You can use the search engine at http://packages.ubuntu.com/ to get them. Choose http://packages.ubuntu.com/ then "Search the contents of packages" and enter the filename. Make sure to choose the right version of Ubuntu too (e.g. Edgy or Feisty).

You may have to repeat this process as n tells you that more files are missing.

It does work though.
